WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
It has been an action-packed few weeks for markets. The US
2026 mid-term elections saw a repudiation of President Trump
with the Democrats now set to take control over both houses
of Congress. The outcome and easing Middle East tensions

saw Brent crude oil fall from just under US$120/bbl to roughly
US$95/bbl, back around the post-Iran-conflict average. The
associated easing in inflation fears has supported a continued
descent in bond yields and rally in risk sentiment – albeit with
significant ‘wrinkles’ around a high US inflation read and an
AI-led pull-back in equity markets that has been compounded
by the sudden resignation of NVIDIA’s CEO.
Many central banks have already begun re-tightening policy

– with the Bank of Canada and Bank of England the latest
additions to the pack – but what comes next will differ widely
across jurisdictions. In the US, the combination of strong
activity and sticky inflation have led FOMC members to more
strongly advocate for further tightening. We have added a
January hike to the December move already in our forecasts,
before a long pause and eventual cuts in 2028.
Europe shares has similar inflation concerns but not so much

strength on the growth front. The ECB sees activity near
trend and is uncomfortable with inflation at 3.8%yr, so we
expect a December hike as insurance. Unlike market pricing
though, we think this will be the last, with energy and
investment-related price pressures easing through 2027 and
scope to ease back towards neutral in 2028. China faces a
very different challenge – not too much demand but too little.
Industrial strength and technology investment are providing

support but we expect policy direction and stimulus will be
needed to turn household demand, with this expected to
come in 2027 and property the most likely catalyst.
The RBA meanwhile followed its September hike with another
in November, but the Governor’s call to ‘take stock’ signals a
December pause and we think the hiking phase is now more
clearly over. We continue to pencil in a move to cuts in the
second half of 2027. While a February hike is not completely

out of the question, policy is clearly restrictive and downside
risks are crystallising. Reflecting this, we have downgraded
our GDP, labour market and housing market forecasts for 2027.
Australia: The housing downturn has been sharper than
expected and is set to spill over more materially to the broader
economy. The scale of these spillovers should become clearer
over coming months, keeping the RBA on hold as downside
risks to growth build. Business investment remains a source

of resilience, led by data centres and renewables, but rising
costs and growing community opposition have seen some
projects shelved. Still, the supply-side picture is constructive,
with strong investment in the market sector driving capital
deepening and productivity gains, albeit uneven ones.
Commodities: The commodities complex was a story of two
halves in October, with sharp gains across energy offsetting
weakness elsewhere and lifting our broadest commodities

index 0.6%mth. Reflecting continued energy-market tightness,
we expect Brent to average US$101/bbl in Q4 and have raised
our Q4 Japanese LNG forecast to US$27.
Global FX Markets: Of late, risks have favoured the US
dollar and FOMC member concerns have been decidedly one-
sided towards higher inflation. The DXY has consequently
continued to test the upper limit of its 18-month range, but
remains inside it.
New Zealand: A busy month has included a tight – and

currently inconclusive – general election, a surprise hold by the
RBNZ in October and a somewhat disappointing Q3 labour
market report. However, the medium-term outlook remains little
changed, with a recovering economy and above-target inflation
likely to lead to further monetary tightening in coming months.
Pacific: Distance from major markets, small domestic
economies and a heavy reliance on imported fuel and food
mean global disruptions tend to reach the Pacific at a higher

cost. However, most Pacific countries face the latest shock
with somewhat better buffers.
United States: Data suggests the US carried strong
momentum in to H2 2026. But questions are being asked over
the profitability of AI deployment, household real incomes are
trending flat to down, and term interest rates continue to weigh.
China: China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3, keeping
authorities on track for 2026, but more importantly offering a
little more time to accelerate domestic demand in to 2027.

Asia: We expect the BoJ to raise its policy rate by 25bps to
1.50% in December, before gradually taking it to 2.0% through
2027. The case for further tightening rests on the evidence that
firms are more willing to raise prices, labour scarcity supports
persistent wage growth, which should in turn support spending.
Europe & UK: The ECB left its key policy rates unchanged in
October. Another hike is forecast in December. Against market
pricing, we expect this will prove the last for the current cycle.

Taking stock

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
All major central banks are now in a rate-hiking phase. The
stance of policy nonetheless varies considerably across
economies, as does the policy outlook.
The Bank of England and Bank of Canada increased their
policy rates for the first time this cycle and the RBA a fifth time

since the last Market Outlook. The pace of increase is generally
moderate: the FOMC and RBNZ paused at their latest
meetings – the RBNZ unexpectedly so – but both are expected
to lift policy rates further. Policy is likely still accommodative
in New Zealand and Canada, consistent with the economic and
labour market slack evident in these economies. In Australia it
is clearly restrictive, as discussed below.
The US is contending with sticky inflation, as evidenced by

recent CPI and labour market data. We have long been of the
view that the US is likely to be structurally more inflationary
than many peers, and concerns about inflation are increasingly
coming to the fore in FOMC members’ comments. Accordingly
we have added another hike in January to our US rates view, on
top of the one in December already forecast (see page 20).
The main, but not sole, driver of the pivot to tightening has been
the energy price shock. That said, exclusion-based core inflation

measures (excluding food and energy) are also above central
bank targets. Market pricing of future central bank moves had
been sensitive to oil prices and the outlook in the Middle East
earlier in the year. That correlation broke down during the bond
sell-off, which was mostly a phenomenon of real yields, but
seems to have  re-emerged in the pricing for policy in 2027.
Spot and daated Brent oil prices have fallen markedly in recent
days as tensions have eased and negotiations appear to have

restarted. This has not affected very near-term pricing of central
bank decisions, and an initial moderation of mid-2027 pricing
retraced after the strong US CPI print and some Fed speeches.
Further along the yield curve, last month’s sell-off in bond
markets has largely reversed. Unlike the increase in yields,
when longer-run market-based measures of inflation
expectations relatively stable, the more recent rally has
involved some decline in these expectations measures. Most of

the adjustment has been front-loaded: the 1-year expectations
derived from swaps have declined 45bps for Australia and
nearly 60bps for the US since the beginning of November.
However, inflation expectations derived for future years have
also moderated. The 5-year-5-year forward measures derived
from swaps and breakevens have both declined, with the latter
noticeably below the 2.5% RBA target midpoint. The same
measures for the US have also declined over this period, but by

less, and remain further above the Fed’s 2% target, consistent
with our view about inflation pressures there.
Global asset market rotation ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

### Page 5

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... while the RBA nears a pause
The rally in long bond markets has occurred at the same time as
equity markets have tended to sell off, particularly in the tech
sector. Another wave of scepticism around the sustainability
of the data centre build-out has formed and found expression

in the equity prices of many semiconductor manufacturers and
other related firms. One possible trigger for the latest sell-off
may have been the release of Anthropic’s prospectus ahead
of its IPO. As well as the more existential risks flagged in the
documents, its stated plans for further data centre investment
have concentrated focus on the scale of the data centre and AI
boom more broadly, and the enormous funding task involved.
The RBA Monetary Policy Board (MPB) hiked at its November

meeting following up the late September hike as expected. As
we noted at the time, the MPB was responding to the apparent
crystallisation of some of the risks that it had flagged back in
August. In particular, the re-emergence of pressures on fuel
prices led to fears of a further wave of pass-through of energy
costs into the prices of other goods and services. The Q3
outcome for underlying inflation outstripped expectations and
Q4 is also likely to be uncomfortably high. With inflation already

materially above target, the RBA is worried that inflation
expectations could be rising, pointing to liaison and business
survey evidence. So far, though, other business surveys suggest
that pass-through has been less severe than the original wave
of price increases at the outbreak of the Middle East conflict.
In the post-meeting media conference, the Governor flagged
that it was a good time to “take stock” of the policy tightening
to date. Both we and the market read this as a signal that the

MPB would pause at the December meeting. The 6:3 split vote
at the meeting also suggests that some external members are
more attentive to downside risks than the internal members.
We think the most likely outcome from here is that the RBA
has ended its rate-hiking phase, and that a cutting phase will
begin in the second half of 2027. There is still a risk of a hike
in February, and we believe the MPB will at least discuss the
option at that meeting. The staff and senior officials remain

sensitive to inflation risks, and have a downbeat view of
supply capacity growth. The latter meant that the RBA had to
upgrade its forecasts for the labour force participation rate in
its However, policy is clearly restrictive, and while there are
upside risks to inflation, there are clearly downside risks, too.
With markets now expecting that the RBA is likely at the end
of its hiking phase, while most peers are expected to have 2–3
hikes to go, short-end yield differentials have narrowed. This

has been reflected in the AUD exchange rate, which has tended
to underperform in recent days. We have tempered our near-
term outlook for the AUD, but note that the TWI remains abourt
4% above the 2022–2024 average, and that this appreciation
will be contributing to the disinflationary impact of tight policy.

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
The housing downturn has been sharper than expected and
is set to spill over more materially to the broader economy.
The scale of these spillovers should become clearer over
coming months, keeping the RBA on hold as downside risks

to growth build. Business investment remains a source of
resilience, led by data centres and renewables, but signs
of strain are emerging, with rising costs and growing
community opposition seeing some projects shelved. The
2026 Annual National Accounts offered a more constructive
supply-side picture, with strong investment across parts of
the market sector driving capital deepening and supporting
productivity, but this has been uneven.
As set out on page 26, we expect the lift in inflation this year

to subside through 2027, with underlying measures easing
back from 3.7%yr currently to 2.7%yr by end 2027 and 2.3%yr
in 2028. Indeed, the deeper housing downturn and associated
spillovers give us greater conviction in this view. The
correction is now the largest nominal price decline on records
going back to 1980 and appears to have accelerated following
the November 25bp RBA rate hike. This is on the back of
dismal dwelling finance approvals outcome which showed

the value of new loans plunged 15.5%qtr in Q3, the biggest
quarterly decline on records going back to 1991.
This downturn has some clear direct effects. The housing
sector, covering both real estate transactions and residential
construction, accounts for around 5–6% of GDP. The drop
in turnover will take about 0.4ppts off GDP growth over
calendar 2026 (captured through the “ownership transfer
costs” component of expenditure GDP) with the downturn also

expected to have a significant drag on dwelling construction
heading into 2027.
Steepest housing downturn in almost 50 years ...
Pat Bustamante, Senior Economist
However, the impact goes well beyond these direct effects.
Falling housing wealth will weigh on household spending,
while the residential building has strong linkages to the rest of
the economy. Residential investment supports activity across
manufacturing, transport and professional services; dollar

for dollar, we estimate its broader economic impact is around
1.5 times that of consumer spending. The flow on impacts via
employment and broader confidence are also substantive.
We have lowered our forecasts for household consumption,
ownership transfer costs and dwelling investment.
Consumption growth is now expected to trough at 1.1%yr
during 2027, from 1.8%yr forecast in our October Market
Outlook, before recovering to 2.7%yr in 2028. Ownership

transfer costs are expected to see a 25%yr decline over 2026,
the sharpest decline since the March quarter 1990, even
larger than what we saw during the pandemic. New dwelling
investment is expected to contract by 1.0%yr in 2027, rather
than grow by 2.1%yr as we expected back in October, before
recovering by 3.2%yr in 2028, down from 4.9%yr. Together
with some flow-on dampening effect on employment, these
revisions lower our GDP growth forecast to just 1.0%yr in

2027, from 1.6%yr, and 2.1%yr in 2028, from 2.5%yr.
We do not expect the slowdown to become immediately
apparent in the data. Consumer spending looks to have been
relatively resilient through the September quarter. Discretionary
services spend has been notably firm in particular, with only
some of this due to a catch-up on travel and tourism spend
disrupted by the Middle East conflict earlier in the year.
Household disposable incomes were still relatively well

supported through the quarter with some lift coming from tax
cuts and the FWC award wage increase. However, our card
tracker data suggests discretionary spending momentum has
faded noticeable over the first half of the December quarter.

### Page 7

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... to drive a sharp economic slowdown ...
The confidence impacts from housing downturn may also be
starting to show. While the drop in consumer sentiment back
to extreme lows has more to do with rate rises and fuel prices,
the weakening in business confidence became more prominent

in October with a notably sharp fall in forward orders. While
this has not had a flow-on impact on employment and capex
expectations to date, these will likely soften if confidence
remains negative and the signal from forward order starts to
be reflected in wider business conditions.
Business investment is still likely to be an important offset, with
data centres and renewables supporting activity. But even here,
some cracks are starting to emerge. Reports of project delays

in NSW and South Australia amid community opposition pose
limited risk to projects already underway but could increasingly
affect the pipeline of proposed data-centre investment (with
the notably cancellation of an IPO for a local operator as well).
Sentiment across the AI sector more broadly has also weakened
with equity valuations dropping, leadership instability at NVIDIA.
Anthropic’s ‘mega IPO’ due November 18 and expected to land
at market valuation of ~US$1trn will be an important test of how

fundamental the shift in sentiment may have been.
Annual National Accounts
The Annual National Accounts (the ‘Annuals’) provide updated
benchmarks and some useful detail including on investment
trends across industries, the capital stock and productivity.
The 2025–26 figures show somewhat firmer economic
activity and business investment than previously estimated,
particularly in 2024–25. The economy grew by 2.3% in
year-average terms, broadly in line with the initial quarterly

National Accounts estimate of 2.4%. However, growth in
2024–25 was revised up by 0.3ppts to 1.6%, largely reflecting
stronger private business investment. Business investment
is now estimated to have increased by 1.1% in 2024–25,
compared with the initial estimate of a 0.2% decline. The
revisions do not materially alter the broader productivity story,
but they do suggest that the economy was growing a little
more strongly but with the same degree of inflation in 2025.

The Annuals also provide a richer picture of the supply side,
including investment by industry and the evolution of the capital
stock, details that are not available in the quarterly accounts.
These data point to a notable lift in investment in the market (ex-
mining) part of the economy, where investment has gone from
around 10% of nominal GDP in to just over 11.5%. Outside of
this, investment has been softer, particularly in the mining sector.
Within the market sector, investment by the IT industry,

increasingly focused on data centres, has risen to its highest
share of nominal GDP since the technology boom of the
1990s. Utilities investment, including renewables and
associated infrastructure, is stronger still, at its highest share
since 1983, while investment by the professional services
industry has reached a record high. Across the non-market
sector (including health, education and public administration),
investment has also held at a slightly higher share of the

economy, driven by the health and public administration
(including defence) industries.
The investment upswing is also beginning to translate into
capital deepening as industries look to rebuild capital stocks
after a period of soft growth. Outside mining, market-sector
capital per hour worked is at a record high and well above its pre-
pandemic level. That is an encouraging development, particularly
given that the sector accounts for 70% of total employment.

There are important divergences beneath the aggregate. Mining
continues to experience capital shallowing, as investment has
been insufficient to cover wear and tear and capital requirements
associated with additional workers. By contrast, the capital stock
in the non-market sector appears to have caught up with the
surge in employment we have seen since the pandemic, with
capital per worker increasing for the first time since the pandemic.

### Page 8

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
8
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
Capital deepening will help support productivity growth
across the economy. The Annuals show that productivity in the
market sector (excluding mining) continues to grow by around
1% in year-average terms, while mining remains a drag on

aggregate productivity (down 0.8% in 2025–26 and almost
30% since 2017).
At the same time, a major recent headwind is easing as the rapid
expansion of the non-market sector slows. This matters for
aggregate productivity. Productivity growth reflects both gains
within industries, through capital deepening, technology and
better processes, and the ‘reallocation’ of resources between
industries with different productivity levels.
Measured productivity in the non-market sector is considerably

lower than across much of the market sector, partly reflecting
measurement issues (output is generally not well measured,
particularly when there are quality changes). As employment
shifted towards these industries, this compositional effect
weighed on aggregate productivity. Our estimates suggest it
has reduced the level of aggregate productivity by a significant
1ppt over the past 15 years and almost 0.3ppts over the three
years to 2025–26.
What drove growth in market-sector productivity?

It wasn’t only capital deepening that drove productivity growth.
Multifactor productivity (MFP), which captures how efficiently
businesses combine labour and capital through changes in
processes, technology, know-how, was the dominant driver of
market-sector productivity growth this year. This gives us more
conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity
growth will improve from its recent lows.
At the industry level, some of those recording stronger

investment are also beginning to deliver stronger productivity
outcomes, although the picture remains uneven.
... as non-mining sector rebuilds capital, productivity
IT stands out at the positive end of the spectrum, alongside
improving outcomes in manufacturing, administrative and
support services, and transport. These are also industries
where the diffusion of AI and other digital technologies could
deliver further productivity gains. Utilities and professional

services should also benefit as stronger investment translates
into greater capital intensity and efficiency.
At the other end, mining remains a major drag, consistent
with the capital shallowing evident in the investment and
capital-stock data. Construction is another notable laggard,
with productivity continuing to deteriorate despite strong
demand from housing, population growth, renewable-
energy infrastructure and data-centre projects. Construction

productivity fell around 2% in 2025–26 and almost 21% over
the 13 years from 2012–13 to 2025–26.
Bottom line
The Annuals provide a more constructive supply-side picture
than the aggregate productivity figures alone suggest. Market-
sector productivity outside mining continues to advance, the
reallocation drag from the expanding non-market sector looks
likely to fade, and capital deepening is strengthening across
important parts of the economy. The strong MFP outcome in

the market sector gives us more conviction that conviction that
the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will
improve going ahead.
The critical question is whether the strong investment
underway in technology, professional services, electricity and
related infrastructure translates into stronger productivity
growth as that new capital is put to work. If it does, the
improvement in capital intensity evident in the Annuals
could mark the beginning of a more durable lift in Australia’s

productivity performance.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
The commodities complex was a story of two halves in
October, with energy prices rising sharply while most
other commodities weakened. Our broadest commodities
index gained 0.6%mth, led by Japanese LNG (+12.8%mth),
with Brent (+6.1%mth) and thermal coal (+2.9%mth) also

higher amid energy-market disruption. Copper edged
0.1%mth lower as AI sentiment weakened, while coking
coal (–3.5%mth) reversed recent gains and elevated yields
continued to weigh on gold (–5.0%mth). Iron ore extended
its slide (–6.1%mth), while aluminium fell 6.5%mth. Lithium
was the clear underperformer, with spodumene down
26.1%mth as environmental approval for CATL’s Jianxiawo
mine renewed oversupply concerns.
Oil supply returns, risk premium remains

Brent crude rose 6.1%mth in October to average US$106.2/bbl
as ongoing strikes and attacks on shipping through the Strait
of Hormuz continued to weigh on sentiment. Inventory buffers
remain depleted, with the EIA estimating OECD inventories
remain 141mb below pre-conflict levels in February. This comes
despite reported crude exports from Gulf producers recovering
to above 85% of year-ago levels in October. Reflecting improving
supply, Dated Brent moved to a discount to the front-month

futures contract at the start of November. Futures prices were
further subdued following the Republican defeat in the US
midterms and market expectations of a less intense US campaign
against Iran. Nonetheless, prices remain elevated, with fewer
tankers available after vessels were redeployed to other markets,
while higher insurance premiums continue to add to costs.
Refined product markets remain tight, with reduced Gulf supply
compounded by Ukrainian strikes on Russian refineries. Together

with higher shipping costs and elevated Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm has roughly doubled since the Strait
closed, with the crack spread averaging around US$60.8/bbl in
the first week of November.
We do not expect a lasting US-Iran agreement until mid-2027.
In the meantime, we assume there will not be a return to full-
scale military intervention. However, periodic Iranian attacks on
ships and facilities are expected to continue, alongside the US

blockade of Iranian exports, maintaining a supply risk premium.
We expect Brent futures to average around US$101/bbl in Q4,
albeit with considerable volatility, before easing gradually as
recovering supply is partly offset by demand from refiners and
governments rebuilding inventories. As the risk premium fades
and the market moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast
Brent at around US$77/bbl in Q4 2027. Refined product supply
is expected to recover more slowly than crude. For additional

analysis, including our October scenarios for further escalation
and an earlier resolution, see here.

COMMODITIES
Qatar’s limited bypass options keep LNG tight
LNG prices continued to climb through October. Damage to
the world’s largest LNG export complex at Ras Laffan in Qatar
and the country’s inability to divert flows around the Strait of

Hormuz continue to weigh on supply. These constraints have
been compounded by stronger competition between Northeast
Asia and Europe for flexible Atlantic Basin cargoes ahead of
the Northern Hemisphere winter, with European gas storage
remaining below seasonal norms. As a result, Japanese LNG
prices rose 12.8%mth in October, averaging US$28.6/mmbtu.
Reflecting the continued acute disruption, we have raised our
December quarter forecast for Japanese LNG prices to US$27.5/

mmbtu. The severity of the Northern Hemisphere winter will
be the key risk to the near-term outlook. Supply recovery is
expected to remain constrained, with prices unlikely to return to
pre-conflict levels until 2028. Domestically, the Australian gas
market remains relatively insulated, supported by the FY2027
Federal Budget’s domestic gas reservation scheme, with an
objective to cap domestic gas prices at around $12/GJ.
Gold caught between yields and haven demand

Gold struggled through October, with prices down 5.0%mth to
average US$4,105/oz. As a non-yielding asset, gold has faced
broader pressure throughout the Middle East conflict as markets
weighed the inflationary implications of elevated energy prices
and the prospect of further central bank tightening. Despite
these headwinds, some pillars of support have emerged. A
meaningful bid has developed around US$4,000/oz, with
dip-buying supporting a recovery in gold ETF demand. Yields

also moved lower at the start of November prior to the US CPI
release as safe-haven demand increased against a backdrop of
macroeconomic, geopolitical and market uncertainty.
With these supports increasingly evident, we expect modest
gains through the remainder of 2026, with a December quarter
average of US$4,200/oz. From there, we expect a period
of consolidation through 2027 as brownfield expansions at
existing operations lift supply in response to recent high prices,

while an elevated Fed Funds rate limits upside for non-yielding
assets. Over the longer term, structural support should persist,
underpinned by growing Asian demand, and central bank buying.
Democrat sweep weighs on copper’s bull case
LME copper prices were broadly stable through October,
edging 0.1%mth lower to average US$14,478/t. The pace of
COMEX inventory accumulation slowed further to 2.9%mth
following reports in September that the US Government’s

decision on Section 232 tariff implementation had stalled
amid concerns higher copper prices could add to already
elevated manufacturing costs. Supply disruptions in Chile
provided more meaningful support, with two fatal accidents
at separate operations resulting in production suspensions in
a region already affected by weather disruptions. Alongside
broader electrification trends and metal shortages outside
the US, these disruptions supported copper through October.

However, a weaker macroeconomic outlook amid elevated
energy costs and a sharp rotation out of the copper-linked AI
trade left prices broadly unchanged over the month.
Several recent sources of support have come under pressure
in early November. The key shift has been the Democratic
sweep of Congress at the US midterm elections. The initial
equity-market reaction saw the AI trade pull back on concerns
that a Democrat-controlled Congress could impose greater

regulatory constraints on AI-infrastructure investment.
Copper’s exposure to the AI buildout made it a casualty of
the broader sell-off, with LME prices falling sharply at the
start of the month. A Democrat controlled Congress has also
reduced the perceived risk of more severe Section 232 tariff
implementation. Against this backdrop, we have downgraded
our near-term LME copper forecast, with prices expected to
average US$14,000/t in Q4. Ongoing electrification trends, AI

and data-centre investment, and metal shortages outside the
US should, however, continue to provide a floor under prices.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
The past month has seen a wave of risk events favouring the
US dollar. Meanwhile, concerns shown by FOMC members
have been decidedly one-sided, their clear message being
that the question is how many more hikes are needed. The
DXY has consequently continued to test the upper limit of

its 18-month range, circa 103, but has held to it.
Underlying the DXY’s movements are several key narratives.
Europe is the first port of call. Since mid-September, euro
has fallen from almost USD1.17 to a low near USD1.11.
Uncertainty surrounding France’s fiscal and economic outlook
was part of that turn, however, most of the move aligns to
concerns amongst market participants over the persistence of
inflation and the resetting of policy rate expectations. Albeit

marginal, expected fed fund rate hikes outnumber those for
the ECB, entrenching short and long-end rate differentials.
Sterling has held up better than euro, falling from around
USD1.36 in September to near USD1.32. But, to our mind, the
chances of the sequence of rate hikes priced by the market for
the Bank of England is low. After November’s policy meeting,
Governor Bailey showed caution over the activity outlook
and a greater willingness to incorporate the recent drop in oil

prices into inflation forecasts than FOMC members have.
Interest rates are not the only comparable favouring the US
dollar; each nation’s growth story is also important. While
Europe and the UK have recently delivered economic outcomes
consistent with a return to trend growth, US data continues
to point to momentum in excess of potential. Moreover, as
discussed on page 19, the pipeline of AI related investment
and funding continues to strengthen, favouring the US.

It is intriguing then that the US dollar has not broken the range
of the past 18 months. Based on this performance, lingering
downside risks for the US economic outlook and the potential
for a more constructive outturn for energy, we remain of the
view that the US dollar is likely to recommence its downtrend
in mid-2027, once this monetary policy cycle definitively ends.
For euro, a slow (but likely volatile) uptrend is anticipated,
from USD1.11 today to USD1.17 at end-2027 then USD1.21

come end-2028. Sterling is similarly forecast to lift from
USD1.32 at present to USD1.38 at end-2027 and then
USD1.41 by late-2028 – seeing broad stability between
euro and sterling. Dissipating global risks over the coming
two years should also result in both euro and sterling lifting
against the Swiss franc – albeit only at the margin.
The primary risk to this view is that appreciation against the
US dollar comes later. Though, as we are only targeting

US dollar support ...

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
There have been several significant developments in
New Zealand since we released the October edition of our
Market Outlook, including a General Election, an RBNZ
policy meeting and some surprising economic data.
Starting with the General Election on 7 November, as

polls had suggested, this proved to be a very tight contest.
Indeed, the ultimate winner of the election will not be known
with certainty until the final official results are published
on 27 November. The theme of the election was one of
fragmentation, with the traditional major parties – National
and Labour – recording their lowest combined vote on record.
As we set out in more detail in our post-election report, on
election night the centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and

ACT won 67 seats. In what is currently expected to be a 131-
seat parliament – thus requiring 66 seats to govern – this gives
the bloc a one seat majority. The centre-left bloc of Labour,
Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57 seats on election night, while
the Opportunity Party – entering parliament for the first time
– won seven seats. Relative to 2023, there was a swing away
from National both to its coalition partners and to the centre left
bloc. Notably, several senior National ministers have not been

re-elected, including Finance Minister Nicola Willis.
While the election night count means that the centre-right is
in the box seat, there are still around 400,000 special votes
to be counted (including around 100,000 overseas votes).
Historically, these votes have significantly favoured the centre-
left parties, especially the Greens, thus reducing the centre-
right’s vote share when the final count is completed. If that
were to occur again, it is quite possible that a party list seat

will transfer from the centre-right (most probably NZ First) to
the centre-left (most probably the Greens).
In addition, the National Party’s current 42 seat haul – seven
seats greater than its normal entitlement based on its party
vote share – would decline should it lose one of several seats
it currently holds by a narrow margin (e.g., the Wairarapa seat,
where the National candidate currently leads by just 139 votes)
without a pick-up elsewhere. The loss of an electorate seat by

National combined with the transfer of a party-list seat from the
centre-right to the centre-left would leave the centre-right with
just 65 seats in what would become a 130-seat Parliament. The
centre-right would thus need the support of the Opportunity
Party to govern – support that that centre-right might seek in
any case given the narrowness of even its current majority.
Whilst less likely, it remains mathematically possible that the
final vote could deliver a centre-left government, supported

by the Opportunity Party. This would occur if NZ First were
A busy month with some surprises ...
to a lose a party list seat to the Greens whilst National were
to lose two electorate seats (it currently holds the Hamilton
East seat by 456 votes). In this situation, the centre-right bloc
would control just 64 seats in a 129-seat parliament. Therefore,
understandably, both the Prime Minister and the leader of
the Labour Party have indicated that substantive coalition

negotiations will not take place until the final count is known.
In other news, the RBNZ surprised financial markets at its
28 October meeting by leaving the Official Cash Rate (OCR)
unchanged at 2.75%. A 25bp increase had been almost fully
priced, and Westpac Economics, along with most forecasters,
had expected a hike to 3% (we had changed our view just a
week prior to the meeting, in part because we thought the
Bank would be reluctant to disappoint market pricing). Instead,

the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) voted 4–3 to hold,
implementing the pause that it had originally signalled in
September and allowing more time to assess the effects of the
50bps of tightening delivered since July.
The close vote reflected differences over the timing of further
tightening rather than disagreement about the broad outlook.
All seven MPC members accepted that additional monetary
restraint would probably be needed, conditional on how the

economy evolved. The three members favouring an immediate
increase placed greater weight on elevated core inflation, the
increasing breadth of price rises and the risk that the latest
fuel and exchange-rate shocks could become embedded
in inflation expectations, wage-setting and firms’ pricing
behaviour. They argued that acting promptly would reduce the
danger of having to tighten more aggressively later.
The four-member majority, including Governor Anna Breman,

preferred to wait. They emphasised that September-
quarter annual inflation, at 3.7%, had been lower than the
Bank expected; wage growth remained subdued; inflation
expectations were stable; and the indirect effects of higher
fuel prices had so far been smaller than assumed. Weak
household spending and residential investment were limiting
firms’ ability to pass higher costs through to customers or
rebuild margins. The majority also saw value in observing the

delayed impact of the two recent OCR increases before adding
further restraint. Global risks also received considerable
prominence. The Committee highlighted geopolitical tensions,
high energy prices, elevated debt, financial vulnerabilities and
uncertainty over whether booming AI investment was justified
by economic fundamentals. It was argued that these factors
could sustain inflation but could also trigger an abrupt global
downturn, reinforcing the case for waiting.

NEW ZEALAND
Darren Gibbs
Senior Economist

Nevertheless, the decision was not dovish. The Bank expected
annual inflation to rise to 4.4% in the December quarter,
reflecting elevated fuel prices and a roughly 3% fall in the
exchange rate since early September. Core inflation had
moved towards the top of the 1–3% target range, while price

increases had become more broad-based. The Committee
therefore retained a clear tightening bias, stating that a higher
OCR was likely, although incoming information could change
the appropriate stance in either direction.
Looking ahead, our baseline forecast remains for a 25bp hike
at the RBNZ’s next meeting on 9 December. However, as we
commented at the time of the October meeting, a rate hike
should not be regarded as a done deal. And the likelihood of

the RBNZ extending the October pause increased following the
release of the labour market reports for the September quarter.
The household survey measure of employment unexpectedly
fell 0.1%qtr, which together with downward revisions to
previous quarters saw annual growth slow to 0.7%yr – just half
of the growth forecast by the RBNZ in the September MPS.
We think that underlying momentum in the September quarter
is somewhat stronger than suggested by the household survey,

with the tax-based Monthly Employment Indicator suggesting
modest growth (and similar growth to the household survey
over the year as a whole). However, the weak signal was
further demonstrated by the unemployment rate, which is less
susceptible to the quarter-to-quarter sample volatility seen in
the household survey. This rose 0.1ppts to a fresh 11-year high
of 5.7%. Moreover, youth unemployment is at levels not seen
since the survey first began in 1986.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Pacific economies are exposed to major external shocks.
Distance from major markets, small domestic economies
and a heavy reliance on imported fuel and food mean global
disruptions tend to reach the Pacific at a higher cost than in
larger, wealthier nations. This time is no different. However,

robust tourism, remittances and commodity earnings combined
with healthy foreign reserves mean most Pacific countries face
the latest shock with somewhat better buffers from the start.
Geopolitical focus has shifted
Geopolitical interest in the Pacific has evolved over time. China’s
growing presence over the past decade saw Australia, New
Zealand, the United States and others deepen their engagement
in the region across infrastructure, development and security.

The headlines may have become quieter, but the strategic
interest has not disappeared. Pacific countries sit at the cross
road of important sea and communication routes and control
vast ocean territories rich in fisheries and potential mineral
resources. Governments have generally used this interest to
their advantage, maintaining the familiar Pacific position of
being “friends to all and enemies to none”.
The latest Middle East conflict brings the Pacific’s vulnerabilities

back into focus. There is limited direct trade exposure, but that
hardly makes the region immune. Higher fuel, freight, insurance
and shipping costs find their way into the cost of imported food,
transport, electricity and the overall cost of doing business.
The regional central banks’ heavy focus on foreign reserves
management, and pegged exchange rate regimes, has provided
something of a buffer to the immediate shocks.
Tonga perhaps captures this exposure best. The National

Reserve Bank of Tonga Annual Report 2026 noted that global
shocks transmit “almost instantly through prices to the local
economy”. The good news is that Tonga has substantial buffers,
with reserves enough to cover 11.5 months of imports.
Tourism is critical for Fiji, and resources for PNG
In Fiji, tourism is doing much of the heavy lifting. Some
762,352 visitors arrived in the first nine months of 2026,
3.7% more than a year earlier, while September recorded a

monthly high of 95,970. Australia accounted for nearly half
of September arrivals, followed by New Zealand. Foreign
reserves provide another cushion, standing at FJ$4.1 billion in
September, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained imports.
The story is slightly different elsewhere. Samoa continues
to receive sizeable tourism and remittance inflows, with
July receipts reaching SAT70.6 million and SAT94.2 million
respectively. Vanuatu had enough reserves in May to cover

14.6 months of imports, although higher petrol and diesel
costs are flowing into transport expenses.
Pacific economies deal with external shocks ...
Shamal Chand, Senior Pacific Economist
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
PACIFIC
PNG has commodities providing a different cushion. Resource
exports support foreign exchange earnings, but households
remain exposed to expensive fuel. The government’s K1 billion
fuel stabilisation package offered some relief before the direct

subsidy was phased out in November. Port Moresby’s maximum
indicative diesel price subsequently moved to K6.69 per litre, a
51% increase after remaining mostly unchanged since March.
Other Pacific economies have their own buffers: gold and
fisheries in Solomon Islands, fishing revenues and offshore
assets in Kiribati and Tuvalu, the Petroleum Fund in Timor-
Leste, and nickel in New Caledonia. But high prices remains
the common theme across the small island countries.

So far, these buffers are holding. The bigger question is how
long will they need to? A prolonged period of expensive energy
and freight, particularly if tourism and/or commodity prices
weaken, would make the next phase more difficult for these
small islands. As such, the risks are skewed to the downside.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
… and have stronger COP31 expectations this time
PACIFIC
The 31st United Nations Climate Change Conference (COP31)
opened in Antalya, Türkiye, on November 9th with a familiar
challenge: turning years of climate commitments into tangible
action. Türkiye is presiding over the summit, while Australia’s

Climate Change and Energy Minister, Chris Bowen, is serving as
President of Negotiations. For Pacific countries, expectations
are high with Australia at the negotiating table.
The Pacific is pushing for immediate action to keep global
warming within 1.5°C, accelerate the transition away from
fossil fuels, improve access to climate and Loss and Damage
finance, and give the oceans a stronger place in climate action.
As UN Climate Change Executive Secretary Simon Stiell

acknowledged at the opening, global climate progress remains
“nowhere near far enough or fair enough.”
These priorities were reinforced through the “Taku Pakasoa
Declaration”, adopted at the Pre-COP31 meetings in Fiji and
Tuvalu in October, alongside calls for stronger implementation,
better access to finance and greater recognition of the ocean.
“Taku Pakasoa” is Tuvaluan and essentially means strength
through unity and collective action.
Early opening day moves

The opening days in Antalya have begun to translate some of
these priorities into the negotiating agenda, although no major
outcome has yet emerged. Discussions on climate finance are
under way, while Chris Bowen has initiated work towards a
Presidency-initiated, Party-driven COP31 decision intended
to provide a focused and practical response to the challenges
facing the summit. Countries are being encouraged to shape
the political package early, rather than leaving the hardest

discussions until the final days.
Energy is also moving to the centre of discussions. Brazil
presented a roadmap aimed at translating global commitments
to transition away from fossil fuels into national plans.
Meanwhile, an IEA report, commissioned by Türkiye and
Australia, finds that electricity could cost-effectively rise from
around 23% of global final energy consumption in 2025 to
33% by 2035. This assessment assumes currently available
technologies and broadly pre-2026 energy-shock prices and

financing conditions. For import dependent Pacific economies,
greater electrification could offer an additional benefit by
reducing exposure to volatile global fuel markets.
The climate finance gap
For Pacific leaders, delivery remains the real test. Fiji’s
Environment and Climate Change Minister, Lynda Tabuya, called
for climate finance to reach “the people who really need it” and
for the ocean to take “its rightful place in the climate agenda.”

The Fund for responding to Loss and Damage illustrates the
challenge. US$822.06 million had been pledged by March 2026,
but its initial operational funding envelope was only US$250 million.
The distinction between money pledged and finance actually
reaching vulnerable communities remains important.
For the Pacific, success will therefore depend less on headline
commitments and more on whether finance becomes easier
and faster to access for adaptation, resilience and clean-

energy projects domestically.
Australia’s test
Australia remains something of a double-edged sword for
Pacific climate interests.
It is a major development and climate-finance partner and
now has an influential role in the negotiations. Yet Australia
also remains a significant fossil-fuel producer and exporter,
creating tension with the Pacific’s push for a credible pathway
to keep 1.5°C within reach.
That makes COP31 an important test of Australian regional

leadership. The summit is increasingly moving from debating
whether the transition should occur towards how it will be
delivered. The fossil-fuel roadmap, electrification push and
renewed focus on climate finance are early steps, but they
remain processes rather than final outcomes.
For the Pacific, COP31 is not about another declaration of
ambition. It is about whether commitments finally translate
into outcomes on the ground.
For Australia, the test is whether its influence at the

negotiating table can help bridge the gap between Pacific
expectations, global commitments and delivery.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
CHINA
China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3 2026, quarterly
growth accelerating from 0.9% in Q2 to 1.2% in Q3. Annual
growth lifted from 4.3% to 4.5% as a result. Though, on a
year-to-date basis, growth edged down 0.1ppts to 4.6%.
The Q3 result keeps authorities on track to achieve their

4.5-5.0%ytd ambition for 2026, and also provides a little
more time to accelerate domestic demand into 2027. Broad-
based stimulus is required because net exports’ contribution
to growth will likely abate in coming quarters now that the
trade surplus is back at record highs, and as many Chinese
firms diversify their risks by expanding domestic and offshore
production networks contemporaneously.
While annual retail sales growth accelerated from 0.4% to

1.1% in September, the Q3 year-to-date growth pulse of 1.1%
is just a quarter of the gain reported a year ago. Automobiles,
construction materials and jewellery have been particularly
heavy detractors over the past year; however, most of the
other sub-categories lack momentum.
As we last detailed two months ago in our September Market
Outlook, this is not because households are cash constrained,
but rather as sentiment is extremely weak and the outlook for

employment and wealth highly uncertain.
Key for wealth is that house prices for new and established
homes continue to decline month-on-month (respectively
0.1% and 0.3% in September), five years into the downturn.
Property investment’s ongoing decline (–20.6%ytd) makes
clear that this persistent weakness in sales and prices is
demand driven.
More broadly, total fixed asset investment has been an outright
negative for annual GDP growth since April. But a recovery is

not beyond either the public or private sector’s capacity. Rather,
across the economy, entities are waiting for direction and
incentive from the Central Government. This is likely to come
by early-2027, to offset the loss of support from trade and as
downside risks to global growth linger.
Helpfully, industrial production continues to grow at a robust
pace, 5.3%ytd, borrowing rates for businesses and households
are low, and authorities are beginning to provide banks with

additional lending capacity.
For larger businesses, officials are also reportedly encouraging
major institutional investors to increase their structural
allocation to domestic equities, building lasting appetite for
new equity issuance to fund future waves of real economic
capacity and income generation.
Patience is a virtue ...
Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
China’s strong position in global technology should provide firms
with the confidence to continue investing ahead of expected
demand. This is particularly the case in areas which are in
structural uptrends, most notably electric vehicles and other green

technology as well as components of high-tech manufacturing
and infrastructure – not to mention the race to develop AI.
The consumer is expected to prove much harder to win over
to the growth story. The relationship between investment
and employment is likely to remain abnormally weak owing to
Chinese firms’ success with robotics and efficiency.
In services, the co-ordination across China’s major technology
firms for the development and adoption of AI is expected to

have a similar effect on employment demand.
The risks on this front are to the downside too. Young workers
today are considerably more educated and skilled than in the
past, and increasingly beholden to a long work week culture. All
else equal, this should lead to high output per marginal worker.
The flow-on benefit to household income from increased
labour demand will also potentially be curtailed by Chinese
firms’ focus on research & development and capacity over

reporting and distributing profits. The pall over the market
also needs to lift to allow households to realise capital gains
from China’s industrial success.
The above means a stimulus-induced recovery in the property
sector and real asset investment by local government entities is,
once again, most likely to prove the catalyst for a turn in household
demand. With a lag, the tightening up of the labour market
and a dividend to consumers from past and current industrial

development could then with a delay accelerate the trend.
... but also a risk

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Economic data received over the past month suggests the
US economy carried strong momentum into the second half
of 2026. That said, of concern for the outlook, questions
are being asked over the profitability of AI infrastructure
and deployment – a critical contributor of recent economic

growth – household real incomes are trending flat to down,
and term interest rates continue to weigh on housing
investment. An offset to these risks may be forthcoming,
however, with the mid-term result a call to President Trump
to make a deal with Iran.
Momentum was certainly broad in Q3, the 3.4% annualised
gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in
household consumption, 12.7% growth in non-residential
investment as well as a robust increase in government

spending. That said, the contribution from business investment
was effectively offset by net exports in Q3, as most of the
componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported.
While strong overall, consumer spending lost momentum
through the quarter, the September PCE data indicating that –
on a real basis – consumption was flat in the month following a
robust 0.5% gain in August and reliant on dissaving. Conscious or
not, this is clearly a concern for many households, with University

of Michigan consumer sentiment holding near historic lows. The
pressure on housing activity is also acute, with first-home
buyer and upgrader serviceability heavily constrained.
Still, aggregate wealth remains near record highs, and consumers
continue to add to their stock of assets. Employment growth also
remains positive and the unemployment rate low. For established
households too, only the marginal dollar spent on housing
investment attracts current elevated interest rates, with the cost

of servicing loans contracted in prior years unchanged.
While questions are beginning to be asked over the scalability
and profitability of businesses at the vanguard of AI and tech
infrastructure, and greater regulation is a risk come 2027 or
2028, financing continues to flow to the sector in scale. Meta,
Oracle and Alphabet have all undertaken successful equity
and / or debt raisings over the past month; and the much-
anticipated Anthropic IPO (expected to be one of the largest

ever) is due within weeks.
Rapid infrastructure development is always at risk of delays
and a change in sentiment. But the capital being made
available to these firms and the urgency the companies are
showing over their opportunity set suggests construction
activity will continue to grow through 2027 and 2028 and
thereafter have a long tail.
AI build out risks justified ...

Ahead of their IPO, Anthropic alone has announced
commitments totalling USD895bn related to computing

According to Bloomberg, just USD70bn of the total spend

The key questions related to US GDP are therefore not the

much will be eaten up by sectoral inflation and, although
these are US-based firms, how quickly the investment drive
will broaden across borders. Current capacity restrictions for
US power and logistics certainly favour a larger share of new
investment increasingly being undertaken outside the US. The

risk of additional regulation also argues for geographic spread.
So, given recent announcements and the flow of capital, and
with the Democrats’ win likely to help ease cost-of-living
pressures early in the next Congressional term, we have
revised up our growth view for the US to 2.4% broadly in line
with 2026. But, come 2028, a return to trend growth seems
probable, arguably with building downside risks.
While inflation risks are therefore likely to dissipate over the

forecast period, in the near term they will remain acute. A
December 25bp hike has seemed inevitable since the October
on hold decision. And, based on the telegraphed fears of
many FOMC members, a further 25bp increase in January has
become more probable than not.
These decisions are responses to the inflation experienced to
date and concern over the potential lagged impact of tariff,
tech and energy pressures experienced by US firms over the
last year. Two hikes would also put off any concerns over

lingering risks for expectations. Note though, on our estimates,
this will set policy at a modestly restrictive setting, which is
not sustainable as growth normalises. So, come 2028, we
continue to look for two rate cuts, with the risk of more.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
The Bank of Japan’s normalisation ...
Illiana Jain
Economist
ASIA
The Bank of Japan’s normalisation cycle has further to run.
We expect the BoJ to raise its policy rate by 25bps to 1.50%
in December, before gradually taking it to 2.0% through 2027.

The case for further tightening is not principally that headline
inflation is high. Rather, the evidence increasingly suggests
Japan has shifted into an environment where firms are more
willing to raise prices, labour scarcity supports persistent
wage growth and nominal wage gains should increasingly
translate into real incomes and household spending. With
inflation anchored at around 2%, a nominal policy rate of 2.0%
would also leave real rates around zero, suggesting policy

would be closer to neutral than outright restrictive.
That is increasingly consistent with the BoJ’s own assessment.
In the October minutes, the BoJ judged underlying inflation
to be at a level broadly consistent with its 2% target, while
medium-to-long-term inflation expectations are around 2%.
More significantly, the discussion has shifted towards the risk
of inflation exceeding target. Risks to prices are now skewed
to the upside, with firms increasingly willing to raise both

wages and prices.
Recent energy-price developments provide an important test
of that change in behaviour. Higher crude prices have naturally
lifted energy inflation, but the effects are spreading beyond
the petrol pump. Higher input costs are increasingly being
passed through to a broader range of consumer prices rather
than absorbed in firms’ margins, while inflation excluding fresh
food and energy is expected to remain above 2%. This broader
pass-through is important evidence that Japan’s inflation

psychology has changed. Services inflation has already
remained above 1% for almost four years, compared with an
average of just 0.3% between 2011 and 2021.
For the BoJ, however, wages remain the critical test of whether
2% inflation can endure. The evidence remains encouraging.
Rengo’s FY2026 negotiations delivered wage increases of 5.08%,
following 5.25% last year. Importantly, the gains have broadened
across the economy: SMEs agreed increases averaging 4.84%,

little different from the 5.10% delivered by larger firms.
Bonus payments provide another important signal. Summer
bonuses increased by around 7%yr, adding to growth in
regular earnings. Attention now turns to winter bonuses, due
shortly. With corporate profits remaining elevated, another
strong outcome would provide an early indication that firms
have both the willingness and capacity to deliver sizeable
wage increases again in 2027.

### Page 22

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
22
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
... has further to run
ASIA
There are already encouraging signs. The BoJ reports hearing
from firms that they intend to raise wages at a similar rate next
year, while its baseline forecast assumes base-pay increases
around the same level as in 2025 and continued nominal wage

growth through 2027. The question is increasingly whether
these nominal gains can finally translate into sustained gains
in household purchasing power.
That is also the key link to consumption. Higher energy prices
have eroded real incomes, leaving private consumption resilient
but lacking momentum despite strong nominal wage growth.
This restraint should ease through 2027. As energy pressures
fade while wages continue rising, real income growth should

strengthen and allow wage gains to feed more clearly into
household spending. The BoJ similarly expects the virtuous
cycle from income to spending to intensify from FY2027,
supporting a return to moderate consumption growth.
Corporate Japan appears well placed to sustain this
adjustment. The operating profit-to-sales ratio is around
6.01%, its highest since the 1960s, including 5.5% for
manufacturers and 6.2% for services. More importantly, those

profits are increasingly being deployed domestically. Governor
Ueda recently noted that more than 70% of Japanese firms
reported investing more actively at home in 2023-24 than
previously, with labour shortages the most commonly cited
motivation. Business investment has exceeded its early-1990s
peak, while bank lending is growing at more than 5%, the
strongest pace in three decades outside 2020.
This combination of strong profitability, investment and

persistent labour scarcity reinforces the prospect that higher
wages can be sustained without placing undue pressure on
firms. It also strengthens the case for further normalisation. At
the October meeting, one opinion argued that firms’ resilience
to higher rates meant the BoJ had entered a new phase in
which it should step up the pace towards neutral, while
another judged the neutral rate to be considerably above 1%.
December therefore looks less like the end of the hiking cycle

than another step towards neutral. Provided wage growth
remains firm, increasingly feeds into real consumption and
keeps underlying inflation around 2%, we expect the BoJ of
Japan to gradually take the policy rate to 2.0% through 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
The ECB left its key policy rates unchanged in October.
Another 25bp hike is forecast in December. Against market
pricing though, we expect this will prove the last for the
current cycle and be reversed in early-2028 once price risks
have subsided.

Evident in the communications that accompanied the October
decision is a sanguine view of current economic momentum,
with activity growth expected to hold near trend and the
unemployment rate stable around its historic lows.
While employment and population growth is currently
subdued, productivity is gradually picking up. “Over the
medium term, a recovery in confidence and real incomes,
driven by gradually declining energy prices and a robust labour

market, should bolster domestic demand”.
“Investment in AI-related activities and government spending
on infrastructure and defence should also continue to support
growth” and spark optimism over the medium-term outlook.
“Simplifying and harmonising rules across the EU’s Single
Market, accelerating the energy transition and completing
the savings and investments union” also holds promise for
business profitability, the public sector’s financial position and

household wealth and consumer choice.
In contrast to their view on activity, the Council is uncomfortable
with the current pace of inflation, its expected trajectory and
associated risks.
Highlighted in the decision statement, headline inflation rose
to 3.8%yr in September as energy inflation surged to 18.9%yr
– diesel and petrol prices reaching new peaks in the month. At
the time of the meeting in October, oil prices were back near
their cycle highs and persistence in services inflation gave

additional cause for concern.
Still, the Council perceived time to be on their side with respect
to responding to inflation.
“While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding,
underlying inflation has remained broadly stable so far and
wages do not show a material response”. “Financing conditions
have [also] tightened”, “which is likely to weigh on inflation
and growth”.
President Lagarde also noted in the press conference that,
between the October and December meetings, a significant

amount of new economic information will be released, allowing
forecasts and risks assessments to be thouroughly tested.
Cautious optimism ...

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
During November to date, risks to the energy inflation outlook
have materially reduced, the price of Brent oil falling from
USD118 at the end of October to USD91 currently.
This abrupt turn largely stems from the market’s worst fears
regarding the Middle East post the US mid-term election not

eventuating as well as a regular flow of anecdotes on indirect
communciation between the US and Iran. An active bilateral
approach to negotiations should see a further large decline in
energy prices, when it occurs. Obviously, this is not a given, but
current circumstances offer promise.
Even if progress towards a deal between the US and Iran
continues over the next month, however, the Governing
Council is likely to take out additional protection against

persistent inflation as a result of the underlying health of the
economy and as, after this hike, monetary policy will only be
marginally contractionary.
In our view though, and against market pricing, another rate
hike in Q1/Q2 2027 seems unneccessary at this stage. Over this
period, inflation risks related to energy supply should subside
at a steady pace. The completion of investment currently being
undertaken may also offer an early disinflation dividend, drawing

inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target. And, with the
labour market broadly balanced, there is also little reason for
concern over a wage-induced uplift in price pressures.
Looking further ahead, there is reason to believe that, come
2028, the ECB will be able to ease back to a neutral stance
despite continued growth at trend.
Simply, another 12 months of investment completions should
improve the economy’s ability to grow without excessive
inflation. An uptrend in the euro towards long-run average

will also be beneficial vis à vis inflation without placing undue
restraint on the region’s growth prospects.
The above notwithstanding, there is a need to remain vigilant of
alternative paths for the Continent. Of immediate concern aside
from the Middle East is the threat posed by Russia’s aggression
against Ukraine and the effectiveness of Ukrainian forces’
counterstrike capability on Russia’s energy infrastructure.
Also, while collectively in much better shape than the US,

the fiscal position of individual nations, particularly France,
requires careful study. Financial conditions have always been
critical for the euro area growth outlook. Currently, this is as
much the case for the public sector as private enterprise.

## Document 458: 【量化专题】天风库存因子系列（一）：库存水平与渠道口径

Source PDF: all report pdf/【量化专题】天风库存因子系列（一）：库存水平与渠道口径.pdf
Report source: 紫金天风
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 31
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

研究报告 | 专题报告  紫金天风期货研究所 
 1 / 31 
紫金天风期货股份有限公司依法已获取期货交易咨询业务资格。 
【量化专题】天风库存因子系
列（一）：库存水平与渠道口径 
2026 年 11 月 10 日

观点小结 
数据来自天风风云数据库中的库存相关序列。本系列从中选取库存水平、社会库存、
港口库存、厂库与库存天数等基础指标，每个品种保留一条代表序列，再按绩效与低相关
原则筛选入选因子。最终得到 22 个因子。本篇聚焦其中库存水平与渠道口径的 6 个：核
心库存、社会库存、港口库存、厂内库存、库存周转天数与原料覆盖天数。 
各因子按统一规则生成每日多空仓位；多数做时序择时，厂内库存做截面多空。基本
面按天风服务器更新时间对齐到交易日并滞后一日；成交按当日收盘价，双边成本万分之
一；评价自 2016 年 1 月起；先验为库存偏高则做空。 
在滚动训练窗口内选参并固定执行；多因子在品种层合成后，有仓品种等权配置。本
篇合成组合夏普约 1.54，年化约 7.29%，最大回撤约 -5.97%。

1 样本与方法 
样本覆盖国内商品期货主要品种。库存序列来自天风风云数据库相关数据，按服务器
更新时间对齐交易日，标准化或变换后滞后一日交易；按当日收盘成交，双边成本万分之
一，评价自 2016 年 1 月起。

作者：聂波 
从业资格证号：F03117695       
交易咨询证号：Z0019358 
联系方式：niebo@zjtfqh.com 
研究助理：王隆 
从业资格证号：F03159955 
联系方式：wanglong@zjtfqh.com 
审核人：李文涛 
交易咨询证号：Z0015640

研究报告 | 专题报告  紫金天风期货研究所
2 / 31
紫金天风期货股份有限公司依法已获取期货交易咨询业务资格。
各因子按统一规则生成每日多空仓位。时序策略根据库存高低做多空择时；截面策略
按当日相对强弱分处两侧。有仓品种等权配置。
单因子在有仓品种上归一得到日度权重；多因子在品种层合成信号，详见后文。在滚
动训练窗口内选参。
2 本篇因子总览
2.1 数据频率与交易日对齐
库存多为周度或更低频观测，策略按交易日定仓与记账。在滚动训练窗口内选参后执
行；总体绩效、合仓与分年结果均按此口径。
慢频库存以天风服务器更新时间为可得时点：仅当更新时间不晚于当日决策截止，该
观测才进入信息集；交易日网格取截止前最新值，标准化或变换后再滞后一个交易日，按
当日收盘成交；公布间隔内仓位沿用既有信号。另以市场总持仓环境做分位稳健性检验。
下表为本篇主口径绩效，评价自 2016 年起。单因子为各自训练窗口选参后的策略路
径；合仓行对各因子信号在品种层合成后，有仓品种等权配置。卡玛为年化收益除以最大
回撤绝对值。

3 分因子构建、逻辑与实证 
3.1 核心库存 
构建：取天风风云数据库中的核心库存相关序列，各品种选用覆盖面尽可能完整的可
见库存口径。按服务器更新时间对齐到交易日后，对库存水平做滚动标准化，并滞后一日
进入时序择时；库存偏高偏空、偏低偏多，有仓品种等权合成为多品种组合。 
下表给出 2016 年起出现过有效非零信号的品种。 
表 2：核心库存品种池（2016 年起有效信号） 
品种数 品种池 
38 LPG、塑料、聚丙烯、PTA、PVC、不锈钢、乙二醇、豆一、尿素、郑棉、棕榈
油、橡胶、沥青、烧碱、热轧卷板、焦炭、焦煤、燃油、玉米、玉米淀粉、玻璃、
甲醇、短纤、纯碱、纸浆、苯乙烯、苹果、菜油、菜粕、螺纹钢、豆油、豆粕、铁
矿石、沪铅、沪铜、沪铝、沪锌、沪镍 
数据来源：紫金天风期货研究所 
交易方式为日度时序择时；变换方式为滚动标准化；先验方向为反向（指标偏高则做
空）。 
经济逻辑：库存偏高意味着近端供给相对宽松，价格承压。覆盖面最广，作为库存主
题的主锚。

3.1.1 总体绩效
主口径（2016 年起）夏普 1.381（表现突出），年化 6.61%，最大回撤 -4.53%。

构建：社会库存为流通环节（贸易商与社会仓库）可见库存；各品种取覆盖面尽可能
完整的口径。滚动标准化并滞后一日后做时序择时，有仓品种等权。
下表给出 2016 年起出现过有效非零信号的品种。
表 5：社会库存品种池（2016 年起有效信号）

数据来源：紫金天风期货研究所
交易方式为日度时序择时；变换方式为滚动标准化；先验方向为反向（指标偏高则做
空）。
经济逻辑：社库升高往往对应渠道累库、现货偏松，与核心库存同向承压，但更贴近
渠道博弈与近月定价。

构建：港口库存覆盖进口品与沿海集散港口库存；各品种优先采用全国或区域汇总口
径，无汇总时再对分港序列加总。滚动标准化并滞后一日后做时序择时，有仓品种等权。

3.4 厂内库存 
构建：同品种多口径时优先更宽覆盖，否则取均值；对齐交易日后滚动标准化并滞后
一日，按截面相对强弱分处多空，有仓品种等权。 
下表给出 2016 年起出现过有效非零信号的品种。 
表 11：厂内库存品种池（2016 年起有效信号） 
品种数 品种池 
19 螺纹钢、焦煤、聚丙烯、尿素、短纤、LPG、烧碱、碳酸锂、纯碱、PTA、铁矿
石、纸浆、工业硅、橡胶、氧化铝、热轧卷板、玻璃、棉纱、豆粕 
数据来源：紫金天风期货研究所 
交易方式为日度截面多空；变换方式为滚动标准化（更长窗）；先验方向为反向（指
标偏高则做空）。 
经济逻辑：厂内库存偏高，通常对应生产端被动累库或需求接货偏弱，近端宽松、价
格承压。覆盖黑色钢厂、化工与农产品样本企业厂库。

整体平滑，持有设定具有稳健性。

3.6 原料覆盖天数 
构建：原料覆盖天数定义为当前原料库存按现有消耗可覆盖的天数；各品种取覆盖面
尽可能完整的口径。滚动标准化并滞后一日后做时序择时，有仓品种等权。

交易方式为日度时序择时；变换方式为滚动标准化（更长窗）；先验方向为反向（指
标偏高则做空）。 
经济逻辑：覆盖天数偏高同样指向近端宽松；中期松紧体制亦纳入观察。

4 品种层信号合成组合 
4.1 如何合成 
每个因子先形成日期×品种的多空信号。本篇合成信号默认采用训练窗口选参。同一品
种上，当日有信号的因子取平均得到合成得分，再按得分绝对值之和归一为品种权重，方
向由得分符号决定。 
本篇共 6 个因子参与合成。合成后夏普 1.544，年化 7.29%，最大回撤 -5.97%。

设定下的峰值夏普直接对标；单因子高点可作为参照，不构成合仓的上界。下图按训练窗

## Document 483: タイ中銀、インフレ加速も景気支援を重視して４会合連続で金利据え置き ～原油高やバーツ安など物価上昇リスクがくすぶるなか、_e3429186

Source PDF: all report pdf/タイ中銀、インフレ加速も景気支援を重視して４会合連続で金利据え置き ～原油高やバーツ安など物価上昇リスクがくすぶるなか、_e3429186.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 28 日

1/3 
タイ中銀、インフレ加速も景気支援を重視して４会合連続で金利据え置き 
～原油高やバーツ安など物価上昇リスクがくすぶるなか、難しい政策運営が続くであろう～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ タイ銀行（中央銀行）は、10 月 28 日に開催した定例の金融政策委員会において、政策金利
を４会合連続で1.00％に据え置くことを決定した。同国では、イラン情勢の悪化による原油
高などを背景にインフレが加速しているものの、内需の弱さを反映して需要面からの物価上
昇圧力は限定的とみられる。 
○ 一方で、世界的なＡＩ関連需要の拡大が一部産業を押し上げているものの、家計債務の大き
さや雇用環境の脆弱さなどが個人消費の重しとなり、景気回復の恩恵は一部にとどまってい
るとの見方を示している。さらに、原油高による対外収支の悪化や洪水対策に伴う財政負担
の増大、通貨バーツ安なども景気や物価に対する不透明感を強めている。 
○ こうしたなか、中銀は足元の金利水準が景気回復を支えるうえで適切との認識を示し、イン
フレの上昇は一時的との見方を維持するなど、景気支援を優先する姿勢を示した。ただし、
エルニーニョ現象やイラン情勢を巡る不透明感に加え、金融緩和の長期化に伴うリスクも意
識されるなか、先行きの政策運営は引き続き難しい局面が続くと見込まれる。

タイ銀行（中央銀行）は、10 月 28 日に開催した定例の金融政策委員会において、政策金利を４
会合連続で 1.00％に据え置くことを決定した。同行は、イラン情勢が緊迫化する直前に開催した２
月会合において、同国経済のコロナ禍からの回復が ASEAN（東南アジア諸国連合）主要国のなかで
も遅れていることに加え、物価の低迷が続いていることなどを背景に利下げに踏み切った。しかし、
直後にイラン情勢が緊迫化したことで状況は一変した。その結果、中銀は金利を据え置く様子見姿
勢を維持している。

### Page 2

2/3 
タイでは、2025 年半ば以降のインフレ率はマイナスで推移していたものの、2026 年４月以降は一
転してプラスに転じている。直近９月の
インフレ率は前年同月比＋2.82％と中
銀の目標レンジ（２±１％）内にとどま
るものの、上限に近付くなどインフレ圧
力が強まっている様子がうかがえる（図
１）。コアインフレ率は前年同月比＋
1.50％にとどまるものの、2023 年５月以
来の高い伸びとなるなど、インフレの動
きが広がりをみせている可能性がある。
とはいえ、コアインフレ率は依然として
低水準にあり、需要面からの物価上昇圧
力も限定的とみられることが、中銀の様子見姿勢を後押ししたと考えられる。 
金融市場においては、原油高が対外収支を悪化させるとともに、政府による家計支援を目的とし
た補助金政策などに伴う財政悪化への懸念も重なり、経済のファンダメンタルズ（基礎的条件）の
脆弱化が警戒されてきた。さらに、タイでは９月中旬以降、豪雨による洪水被害が広がり、政府は
洪水対策を目的に財政支援を拡充した
ため、公的債務残高のＧＤＰ比は法定上
限（70％）に近付いている（注１）。こう
した事情も重なり、通貨バーツ相場は調
整の動きを強めている（図２）。タイで
は、伝統的に金取引が活発であり、バー
ツ相場は年明け直後にかけて金価格に
連れ高する形で強含んだ。しかし、足元
では金価格が調整の動きを強めており、
こうした動きもバーツ安を後押しして
いる可能性がある。バーツ安は輸出競争
力を高める一方、輸入物価の上昇を通じてインフレ圧力を増幅させることが懸念される。とはいえ、
足元では景気の不透明要因が山積しており、中銀にとっては様子見姿勢を維持せざるを得ない事情
もうかがえる 
会合後に公表した声明文では、今回の決定が「全会一致（出席した６名の政策委員が据え置きを
支持し、１名が欠席）」であったことを明らかにした。タイ経済については「ＡＩ（人工知能）関連
の好循環が景気の勢いを増している」ものの、「経済成長率は潜在成長率を下回り、景気拡大の恩恵

### Page 3

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。
3/3
は一部に集中している」との認識を示した。一方で、物価動向については「供給要因を理由に一時
的な上昇が見込まれる」とした。信用動向については「全体的な伸びは加速している」ものの、「中
小企業向け融資は引き続き縮小している」ほか、「中小企業や家計向け融資の質を注視する必要が
ある」との認識を示した。そのうえで、中銀の政策運営について「緩和的な金融政策と的を絞った
金融措置は景気回復を下支えしている」と評価しつつ、「金利を据え置き、イラン情勢やエルニーニ
ョ現象によるインフレリスクの影響を注視する必要がある」とした。
具体的には、「2026 年の経済成長率は＋2.4％、2027 年は＋2.1％になる」との見通しを示す一方、
「その恩恵はＡＩに関連する製造業やサービス業の企業・労働者に集中している」とした。個人消

費については「家計債務の大きさ、脆弱な雇用環境、低所得世帯を中心とする購買力の弱さが重し
になっている」との見方を示した。加えて、「 原油高が外国人観光客数の足かせになる」としたほ
か、「 全体としての経済成長が潜在成長率を下回るなかで、中小企業は依然事業環境への適応や競
争激化という困難に直面している」との認識を示した。
物価動向については「2026 年のインフレ率は＋2.1％、2027 年は＋1.3％になる」との見通しを
示し、「エネルギー価格やエルニーニョ現象の影響で 2027 年初めにかけて上昇が続く」一方、「そ
の後は構造的な制約要因による低成長や内需の弱さを反映して鈍化する」とした。コアインフレ率
も「2026 年は＋1.2％、2027 年は＋1.3％になる」との見通しを示すとともに、「中期的なインフレ
期待は依然として目標レンジ内に定着している」とした。ただし、「イラン情勢の動向、補助金財政
の余力低下、企業によるコスト転嫁の影響、中期的なインフレ期待による上振れリスクを注視する」

とした。
バーツ相場については「ＦＲＢ（米連邦準備制度理事会）による引き締め観測や原油高が重しに
なっている」とした。一方で、「国際的な金利上昇の動きにあわせてタイでも長期金利が上昇してい
る」ものの、「民間部門の借入コストは概ね安定している」ほか、「 大企業向けをけん引役に信用動
向は回復しているが、中小企業向け融資は縮小している」、「中小企業や低所得者層をはじめ家計の
債務返済能力を注視する必要がある」との認識を示した。
先行きの政策運営については「物価安定と持続可能な経済成長、金融安定の維持を目指す」との
従来からの考え方を繰り返した。そのうえで、「足元の金利水準が景気回復を支えるうえで適切」と
して、景気支援を優先する考えを示した。物価動向については「供給要因による物価は上昇してい
るが、今後もインフレ見通しや関連するリスクを注視する」と８月の前回会合と同じ文言を繰り返
すなど、インフレが一時的なものにとどまるとの認識を維持している。一方、今回は「低金利が長
期的なマクロ金融安定に及ぼす影響」にも言及するなど、金融緩和の長期化に伴うリスクにも一定

の警戒姿勢を示した。とはいえ、先行きはエルニーニョ現象の影響が長期化する懸念が高まってお
り、世界経済や国際金融市場を巡る不透明要因も山積するなか、政策の舵取りは引き続き難しい局
面が続くと考えられる。
以 上

## Document 520: 中間選挙後の対米投資の注目点

Source PDF: all report pdf/中間選挙後の対米投資の注目点.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-11-11
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 11 月 11 日

調査レポート

中間選挙後の対米投資の注目点

～「5,500 億ドル合意」は引き続き案件選別とモニタリングに留意を

調査部 研究主幹 中塚伸幸 
〇トランプ政権下で米国の直接投資受入れは相応に進展しており、日本からの投資も同様である。国際収支で
は「グリーンフィールド投資」の把握は難しいが、他のデータ等を勘案するとこちらも堅調である模様だ。 
〇政府間で合意された「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブ」、すなわち 5,500 億ドル対米投資は、公表案件の事業
規模で約 2 割の進捗だが、資金拠出である契約締結済み協調融資額では 2%程度である。 
〇中間選挙後もトランプ政権の貿易・投資政策には今のところ大きな変化はなさそうだが、予断を許さない。
「5,500 億ドル投資」については、今後、米側から対応加速を求められても、採算性の審査基準を維持し、事
後モニタリングも着実に行うことが肝要。円安影響はマネージ可能とみられるが、市場動向に留意は必要。 
1. はじめに 
米国の中間選挙では、トランプ大統領の共和党が大敗した。2 期目のトランプ政権は関税による保護主義的な
貿易政策を推進するとともに、他国による対米直接投資を歓迎してきた。わが国も関税交渉の一環として「日米戦
略的投資イニシアティブ」による 5,500 億ドルの対米投資に合意し、これを実行中である。  
上下両院で民主党が多数となっても、今のところトランプ政権の貿易・投資政策が大きく修正される可能性は
小さいと思われるが、予断を許さない。今後の動向を注視する上での前提として、対米投資の現状を整理する。 
2. 米国への直接投資流入の現状 ～国際収支で全貌の確認は難しいが、AI 関連は堅調な模様 
まず、米国への直接投資流入額を国際収支統計から、株式資本（ネット）、再投資収益、負債性資本（ネット）
の合計額でみると、図表 1 のように、2025 年は前年からほぼ横ばい、2026 年上半期は前年比増加となっている。 
図表 1 米国への直接投資の流入額

（出所）BEA ”U.S. International Transactions and Investment Position”

### Page 2

ただし、トランプ大統領が歓迎する工場設備の新設・増設など、いわゆる「グリーンフィールド投資」の動向は、
このデータでは十分に把握できない。国際収支の「再投資収益」は米国内の外資法人の内部留保増加額（利益
蓄積額）であり、また「株式資本（ネット）」も多くの部分は外国資本による既存米国企業の買収額等であるため、
直接的に設備増強に結びつくものは限られる。 
グリーンフィールド投資の動向をみる上では、Financial Times が提供する fDi Markets のデータがあるが、
これを引用している FRB の 2026 年 6 月のレポート 1によれば、2025 年の国外から米国へのグリーンフィールド
投資（公表ベース）の件数は 2024 年比ではやや減少している。 
一方、同じく fDi Markets のデータを引用している UNCTAD の 2026 年 10 月のレポート 2によれば、2026
年上半期の米国へのグリーンフィールド投資（公表ベース）の金額は 2,000 億ドルを超え、前年同期比では 5%
減ながら、半期としては過去最高だった 2025 年上半期に次ぐ高水準となっている。このうち、40%以上は半導体
やデータセンターなど AI 関連の投資で、個別プロジェクトで最も金額が大きいのは TSMC の工場建設（2025 年
は 1,000 億ドル、2026 年は 765 億ドル）とのことである。すなわち、必ずしもトランプ政権の政策を反映して投資
流入が足元で大きく増えているわけではないが、AI 関連を中心に相応の対米投資は行われている、といえそう
である。 
3. 日本からの対米直接投資 ～ネットフローでは大きな変化ないが、グロスでは増加続く 
日本の国際収支統計から対米直接投資の動向を確認すると、図表 2 のように、ネットのフロー額（投資実行額
から投資回収額を差し引いた金額）では今のところ 2025 年以降に大きな変化はみられない。もっとも、グロスで
みた投資実行額は着実に増加が続いており、2025 年は 42.7 兆円と、2014 年以降で過去最高であり、2026 年
1～8 月累計も前年比 11%増となっている。ただ、同時にグロスの回収額も増加しているため、ネットでは 2025 年
は前年比減、2026 年 1～8 月累計でも前年比微減となっている。 
図表 2 日本の対米直接投資フロー

（出所）国際収支統計 （注）26 年は 1-8 月の累計

1 Kallen, C.(2026) ”Recent Developments in Foreign Direct Investment in the United States,” FEDS Notes, June 29. 
2 UNCTAD(2026), Global Investments Trends C. Monitor, No 51, October.

### Page 3

なお、上述の米国の国際収支と同様、ネットフローには在米日系企業の再投資収益（内部留保蓄積）や企業
買収などが含まれ、グリーンフィールド投資はこのうちの一部にとどまる。 
2025 年以降の個別案件では、ソフトバンクによるオープン AI への出資（2025 年 300 億ドル、2026 年 300 億
ドル）、日本製鉄の US スチール買収（2025 年 142 億ドル）、日本生命によるレゾリューションライフの買収（2025
年 84 億ドル）などの大型案件が実行されているほか、投資計画では、トヨタの工場設備増強（今後 5 年間で最大
100 億ドル）なども発表されている。 
また、2025 年以降の主なグリーンフィールド投資案件では、fDi Markets のデータを引用した JETRO の
2026 年 7 月のレポート 3によると、データセンター投資としてソフトバンクグループ（2 件で計 14 億ドル）および
NTT データ（2 件で計 10 億ドル）の案件があるほか、ソフトバンクグループによるオハイオ州でのガス火力発電
（330 億ドル、後述の「5,500 億ドル対米投資合意」の対象案件）、信越化学の石油化学品案件（34 億ドル）、JST
の電子部品案件（5 億ドル）、日立エナジーの変圧器案件（5 億ドル）、日東電工の化学品案件（4 億ドル）などが
挙げられており、AI 関連、電力関連を中心に、民間企業による対米投資は総じて活発といえるであろう。 
4. 日米合意「5,500 億ドル対米投資」の進捗状況 ～事業規模ベースでは約 2 割の進捗 
2025 年 9 月に覚書を締結した「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブ」、いわゆる 5,500 億ドルの対米投資は、民間
企業による投資を政府が約束したものではない。図表 3 の左上部分に示されるように、本件は国際協力銀行（以
下 JBIC）による出融資と日本貿易保険（以下 NEXI）の保証付きの民間金融機関融資を原資として、日本側の
投資法人を通じ、米国での事業主体である SPV（特別目的事業体）に資金を供給するスキームである。 
図表 3 「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブ」のスキーム

（出所）財務省国際局「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブの現状報告」 P5 
（財政制度等審議会 財政投融資分科会 2026 年 9 月 18 日 資料）

3 伊藤博敏「米国データセンター最新事情(2) 電力インフラ周辺に広がる事業機会」JETRO、2026 年 7 月 6 日、表 2

### Page 4

すでに、図表 4 に示す 7 件のプロジェクトが公表されているが、7 件の事業規模の合計額は最大の場合で
1,300 億ドルとなっており、これは 5,500 億ドルの 23.6%に相当する。ただ、日米の合意は「資本コミットメント
（Capital Commitment ）」であるので、合意額の進捗という点では、協調融資額（JBIC の出融資と NEXI 付保
の民間金融機関融資の合計額）でみるほうが適切かと思われる。その場合、協調融資の契約締結額は現状 110
億ドルで、5,500 億ドルの 2%程度である。 
協調融資による資金供給はプロジェクトの段階に応じて行われ、「初回投資では総見込額の 3%～1 割程度 4」
とのことなので、協調融資額は今後、増額されていくことになろう。また、現在検討中の投資案件が順次公表され
ていくものと見込まれる。ただ、現時点の出融資契約額は合意額の 2%であり、まだ先は長いといえよう。 
図表 4 「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブ」の公表案件

（出所）経済産業省、財務省、JBIC、NEXI 等の公表資料から作成  
（注）協調融資額は契約締結済みの融資限度額、JBIC の出資額も含む

5. 「5,500 億ドル対米投資」の今後の注目点 
今後の対米投資のうち、「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブ」（5,500 億ドル投資）について今後を展望すると、以
下の点に引き続き留意が必要であろう。 
(1) 採算性の確保 ～当然ながら案件の厳選と事業開始後のモニタリングが重要 
本スキームのカギは、言うまでもなく投資プロジェクトの採算である。案件採択のプロセスは、日米双方が参加
する協議委員会での協議を経て、米･商務長官を議長とする投資委員会が審査して大統領に推薦し、大統領が
決定する、という流れであり、協議委員会の場で日本側の意向も反映される、とされている。

4 財政制度等審議会･財政投融資分科会 2026 年 9 月 18 日 資料 財務省国際局「日米戦略的投資イニシアティブの現状報告」

### Page 5

合意履行期限である 2029 年 1 月まで残り 2 年 2 カ月であるが、現時点の公表ベースでは、進捗は限られる。
万が一、トランプ大統領が投資合意の履行を急かすことになったとしても、採算性の評価基準を緩めることがない
ようにしなければならない。また、公表済み 7 件のうち 3 件がデータセンター向けを念頭においた発電案件であ
るが、中間選挙後にデータセンターをめぐる環境が変化し得る点にも留意が必要であろう。 
 さらに、投資実行後の事業モニタリングも案件選別と同様に重要である。この点については、専門家レベルで頻
繁に協議を行うなど着実にフォローアップを実施する旨が財政制度等審議会･財政投融資分科会や大臣の国会
答弁で示されている。実効性のあるモニタリングが求められる。 
(2) JBIC、NEXI のリスク許容力 ～運用次第だが、必ずしも過大なリスクテイクではない 
投資資金回収の最終的なリスクを負うのは、出融資をする JBIC と民間金融機関融資を保証する NEXI であ
る。いずれも政府機関であり、かりに回収に支障があれば財政負担になりかねない（なお、JBIC の「償還確実
性・収支相償性の原則」および NEXI の「収支相償性の原則」がそれぞれ遵守される点は、財政制度等審議会･
財政投融資分科会や大臣の国会答弁等で表明されている 5）。 
 5,500 億ドルを両者がどの比率で負担するかは案件ごとの判断だが、これまでの公表分では、約 3 分の 1 が
JBIC、約 3 分の 2 が NEXI 保証付き民間融資となっている。かりに、5,500 億ドルを 1 対 2 の比率で按分し、1
ドル＝158 円で換算すると、JBIC は 29 兆円、NEXI は 58 兆円となる。 
これは、図表 5 に示すように、JBIC 全体の出融資承諾額が 2025 年度で 2.5 兆円（米国向けは 1.3 兆円）、
同じく NEXI 全体の保険引受実績が同 8.4 兆円（米国向けは 0.6 兆円）であるのと比較すると、決して小さい金
額ではないが、両社の純資産、業務内容に鑑みると、必ずしも過大なリスクテイクというわけではないだろう。繰り
返すが、案件選別とモニタリング次第といえる。 
図表 5 JBIC と NEXI の事業実績

（出所）JBIC データブック、NEXI 年次報告書

5 財政制度等審議会･財政投融資分科会 2025 年 12 月 5 日 説明資料 財務省理財局

### Page 6

(3) 為替リスク ～円安リスクはマネジブルか 
投資資金はドル建てであり、場合によっては本件が為替に円安方向の影響を与える可能性がある。この点、政
府によれば、資金供給はプロジェクトの進捗に応じて段階的に行うことから、「一度に多額の、円安を招くような規
模の資金調達は想定していない」（大臣答弁）6と説明されている。 
ドル資金の調達は円投のみに依存するものではなく、JBIC については、政府保証付きドル債の発行や（JBIC
は 2025 年度末で 292 億ドル（4.6 兆円）の政府保証付きドル債発行残高があり、2026 年 7 月にも 15 億ドルの
同債券を発行している）、融資額の原則 5 割を上限として外為特会からのドル資金借入もあり得る、としている。ま
た、民間金融機関融資については、一部案件では外銀 2 行の参加も表明されており、外銀の調達は円投によら
ない可能性も高い。したがって、円安リスクについてはある程度マネージ可能であるように思われるが、市場動向
をにらみつつ対応することが必要であろう。 
以 上
